## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Score is minimized. Delivering items from one half decreases score by 1 (good), from the other half increases by 1 (bad).
- We need robots to: find the source (black floor), pick up items from the correct half (e.g., BLUE wall side), return to nest (white floor), and deliver.
- Key perceptions: floor color (black=source, white=nest, gray=transit), wall colors (red=nest wall, blue/green=source halves).

**Champion analysis:**
- State 0: GoToColor(clr=1=red) → go toward red wall (nest side). Transitions: if sees color 4(yellow) go to state 2, if >2 neighbors go to state 0 (self-loop? No, destination 0 from state 0 isn't allowed... wait, destination index 0 means first OTHER state). With 4 states leaving state 0, others are [1,2,3], so destination 0=state1, 1=state2, 2=state3.
- Actually let me re-read: "destination, indexed among the OTHER states only. With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2."

So for 4 states, leaving state 0: others are [1,2,3], D=0→state1, D=1→state2, D=2→state3.
- State 0 (GoToColor red): n0x0 D=2→state3, n0x1 D=0→state1
- State 1 (Exploration rwm=41): n1x0 D=0→state0, n1x1 D=1→state1(invalid!), n1x2 D=3→state3

Wait, leaving state 1, others are [0,2,3], D=0→state0, D=1→state2, D=2→state3.
- n1x0 D=0→state0, n1x1 D=1→state2, n1x2 D=3→state3? No, D=3 is out of range (max index is 2 for 3 other states). Actually with 4 states leaving state 1, others=[0,2,3], so D=0→state0, D=1→state2, D=2→state3.
- n1x2 D=3 would be invalid... but the champion is working, so maybe D indexes are 0,1,2 mapping to the 3 other states [0,2,3].

I'll trust the champion works and just copy it verbatim.

Let me write the actual command lines now, being very careful with the destination indexing.

For N states, leaving state I, the "other states" are listed in order [0,1,...,I-1,I+1,...,N-1], and D indexes into that list.

**2 states:**
- Leaving state 0: others=[1], D=0→state1
- Leaving state 1: others=[0], D=0→state0

**3 states:**
- Leaving state 0: others=[1,2], D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- Leaving state 1: others=[0,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- Leaving state 2: others=[0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1

**4 states:**
- Leaving state 0: others=[1,2,3], D=0→state1, D=1→state2, D=2→state3
- Leaving state 1: others=[0,2,3], D=0→state0, D=1→state2, D=2→state3
- Leaving state 2: others=[0,1,3], D=0→state0, D=1→state1, D=2→state3
- Leaving state 3: others=[0,1,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state1, D=2→state2

Now let me verify the champion indexing makes sense:
- State 0 (4-state, leaving s0): others=[1,2,3]
  - n0x0 D=2→state3, n0x1 D=0→state1 ✓
- State 1 (leaving s1): others=[0,2,3]
  - n1x0 D=0→state0, n1x1 D=1→state2, n1x2 D=3→... wait D=3 is invalid (max D=2 for 3 others)
  
Hmm, n1x2 D=3... That seems wrong. But the champion scored -1.29 so it must be valid. Let me re-read the rules.

"--n<I>x<K> D : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only."

With 4 states, leaving state 1, others=[0,2,3] → D=0→state0, D=1→state2, D=2→state3. So D can only be 0,1,2.

But the champion has n1x2 D=3. This might be a bug in the champion or D might just be clamped. Since the champion works and scores -1.29, I'll copy it verbatim and not worry about it.

Actually, let me reconsider some things. The champion uses clr=6 and clr=2. In the arena:
- Red wall = nest side → clr=1 (red)  
- Blue walls = one source half → clr=3 (blue)
- Green walls = other source half → clr=2 (green)

But what about clr=4,5,6? Those might be yellow, magenta, cyan. The arena doesn't mention those colored walls, so clr=4,5,6 might be robot LED colors. The champion state 0 checks for clr=4 (yellow LEDs) and state 2 uses clr=1 for a camera condition.

Actually looking more carefully at the champion:
- State 0: GoToColor(clr=1=red) → going toward red wall (nest)
- State 2: GoToColor(clr=6=cyan?) 
- State 3: GoToColor(clr=2=green) → going toward green wall (source)

The LED colors set by `--cle` might be what robots see with clr in conditions. So clr=4=yellow (LEDs set by other states with cle=4), clr=5=magenta (cle=5), clr=6=cyan (cle=6).

The champion state 0 has cle=5 (magenta), state 1 has cle=5 (magenta), state 2 has cle=4 (yellow), state 3 has cle=5 (magenta).

So in state 0, it moves toward red wall (clr=1), and if it sees yellow LED robots (clr=4, which are robots in state 2), it goes to state 3. If it has many neighbors it goes to state 1.

State 2 (GoToColor clr=6 cyan): But what has clr=6 in the arena? Maybe it's trying to go toward cyan LEDs (robots with cle=6), but no state sets cle=6. So it might be looking for a cyan wall, which doesn't exist... which means the behavior is essentially random movement since no cyan objects exist?

Actually I think the arena walls could have different colors than I assumed. Let me reconsider the color mapping. The problem says clr in {1,2,3,4,5,6} for GoToColor. If these are:
1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

Then the arena has: red wall (nest), blue walls and green walls (source halves). So clr=1 goes to nest, clr=2 goes to green source, clr=3 goes to blue source.

State 2 with clr=6 (cyan) would be going toward cyan objects, which could be cyan LED robots (if any have cle=6) or no objects exist. This seems like it might be mostly random/ineffective movement.

State 3 with clr=2 (green) makes sense - going to green source.

Maybe the champion works by: state 3 picks up items from green source (score decreases), then state 0 (GoToRed) returns to nest, and the rest is coordination noise.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The champion (-6.24) has a clean 4-state structure: GoToGreen→on-black→GoToRed→on-white→GoToGreen, with an exploration fallback (state 2) triggered by seeing green LEDs or black floor, and a duplicate GoToGreen state 3 also cycling back. Key insight: state 3 (clr=2, cle=4) mirrors state 0 and both feed back to exploration; the core loop is s0↔s1 via floor color. I'll exploit by tuning probabilities and the exploration rwm, and explore by adding more transitions and trying slightly different topologies.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
The champion (-7.17) is very stable with four variants tied at the same score, all sharing the core s0↔s1 loop (GoToGreen↔GoToRed via floor color) plus an exploration state 2 and a redundant GoToGreen state 3. The 4-state topology with exploration in state 2 clearly dominates 2- and 3-state versions. I'll exploit by fine-tuning probabilities and rwm around the champion, and explore by adding a 4th transition in state 1 (to detect white floor sooner), varying the exploration state behavior, and trying attraction-based coordination.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 14 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.51 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
